#323. Steam Deck performance gets a reality check with Valve's new FPS data

Benchmark tools for generic gaming rigs could be coming soon.

Since Valve announced its Steam Deck Verified program in 2021, players have found that a game’s “Verified” status doesn’t always match how well it actually runs on the handheld. Now, new Steam Deck frame rate data being shared by Valve itself can help players dig deeper into the performance they can expect from many Steam Deck games.

Valve has been collecting anonymized frame rate data from Steam players who opt in since February. Yesterday evening, the company announced it has started sharing that data on many Steam Store pages as averages and histograms for both the Steam Deck and Steam Deck OLED.

Valve acknowledges that players enabling VSync or frame rate limits can bias the collected data somewhat and that the measurements don’t account for custom in-game settings that Steam Deck users may tinker with to get a game running. Regardless, Valve says it has nonetheless “found that the framerate data consolidates around relatively narrow ranges.”

This kind of data can be extremely useful for distinguishing between the wide range of performance levels that can exist among “Verified” Steam Deck games. Both Cyberpunk 2077 and Skyrim: Special Edition earn the same Steam Deck Verified badge, for instance, but the former sees average Deck frame rates in the 30–40 FPS range, while the latter usually achieves 50–60 FPS on the hardware.

Valve’s frame rate data is even more useful for games that the company insists are “Unsupported” on Steam Deck because their “graphics setting cannot be configured to run well” on the hardware or because Valve is “still working on adding support for this game.” Despite warnings like these, games like Starfield, Silent Hill 2, and Assassin’s Creed Syndicate, to name just a few, all show extremely playable frame rates reported by actual Steam Deck users. Assassin’s Creed Syndicate in particular approaches a 60 FPS average on the Steam Deck OLED hardware despite its “Unsupported” status.

Frame rate data can also be useful for judging the single-player modes of games whose multiplayer doesn’t work under SteamOS. Watch Dogs 2, for example, is officially Unsupported on Steam Deck because of anti-cheat compatibility issues, but Steam Deck players report averages of 40+ FPS on the game’s single-player modes.

Of course, collecting and averaging frame rate data for the Steam Deck(s) is made simpler by their set hardware configuration. When it comes to communicating expected frame rates for the wide range of hardware in more generalized gaming PCs, Valve says it is “always looking for new ways to help developers understand the experiences customers are having with their games.”

Back in April, JSON files buried in the Steam client pointed to a potential Steam “Framerate Estimator” tool that could let users “Select an App and a PC config to get a chart of estimated framerates, based on the framerates of other Steam users,” according to strings contained within. That could be incredibly useful for Steam users who want to go beyond the listed Minimum and Recommended hardware requirements without relying on shady-looking third-party FPS-estimator websites. In the meantime, is sharing average frame rate data for the fixed-configuration Steam Machine too much to ask?

#322. NVIDIA shareholders are suing the company and CEO Jensen Huang

The company’s shares have plummeted in response to the exec’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The unfolding drama around NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang's affair with a woman now accused of being a spy for the Chinese government may be getting its first courtroom chapter. A shareholder in the computing giant has sued accusing NVIDIA and Huang of misleading investors about the nature of the CEO's relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at the Taiwanese company Megaspeed who is now suspected of being an espionage asset for China. Huang is a common surname; The pair are not related.

Mark Kistenmacher filed the complaint and is seeking class action status for anyone who purchased NVIDIA shares between October 9 and November 8. The first date is when The New York Times published a story on NVIDIA and Megaspeed detailing a connection between the pair, which was rebuffed by NVIDIA as a routine business relationship; the second date is when the NVIDIA founder admitted to an affair and the company publicly disclosed that it was launching its own investigation into whether its top exec had been targeted by a foreign intelligence operation.

In the interim, The New York Times published an in-depth feature on October 29 claiming that the NVIDIA CEO had become the subject of a suspected Chinese intelligence action. The piece cites NVIDIA representative John Rizzo, who said, "Jensen's interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year." This statement, the lawsuit alleges, was not true.

The company's shares have dropped in the fallout of the allegations, with NVIDIA on track to lose more than $470 billion in market value over the past two days. "As a result of Defendants' wrongful acts and omissions, and the precipitous decline in the market value of the Company's securities, Plaintiff and other Class members have suffered significant losses and damages," the complaint states.

#320. GTA 6 preorders are live: Where to buy for PS5 and Xbox

It's the last call for your "we got X before GTA 6" memes. Preorders for Grand Theft Auto 6 are live ahead of its Nov. 19 release. The highly anticipated game is available in two editions for PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X/S: an $80 standard edition and a $100 Ultimate Edition. Both come with a slew of preorder bonuses if you purchase them before launch.

Physical copies of GTA 6 are available for preorder at Amazon, Best Buy, GameStop, Target, and Walmart. Digital copies are available through the PlayStation Store, Microsoft Store, and Rockstar Games Store.

The $100 GTA 6 Ultimate Edition features "an exclusive collection of premium vehicles, weapons, apparel, and action," according to a press release from developer Rockstar Games. It's previewing these extras on its website, which include:

Exclusive hairstyles, makeup, and nail art

Access to two exclusive vehicle mod shops and a clothing store

A '67 Vapid Dominator Buggy vehicle with a garage

A raid mission for the PTT Youngin$ Illegal Goods Store, a gang compound

A side mission to find and restore classic cars

The GTA 6 Ultimate Edition is only available for preorder on digital through the PlayStation Store, Microsoft Store, and Rockstar Games Store.

All players who buy GTA 6 before Nov. 20 will get a bonus Vintage Vice City Pack, which Rockstar describes as "a collection of items that flash back to when the neon burned brightest." There's a yellow ’55 Vapid Stanier vehicle with its own garage; special outfits and hairstyles for Jason and Lucia, the game's two protagonists; and a palm tree weapon pattern.

Those who preorder digital copies will also get a free month of GTA+, a membership service that unlocks GTA Online bonuses and a library of older Rockstar titles. It normally costs $7.99 per month.

You'll only get the free month of GTA+ if you preorder the digital version of GTA 6. Physical copies of the game just come with a download code in the case instead of an actual disc, so the biggest reason to buy that version instead is if you want to collect the box art.

Players who preorder a digital copy of GTA 6 will be able to preload the game on their consoles starting Nov. 12. All major retailers plan to ship physical copies, which will also arrive on Nov. 12 and can be preloaded ahead of launch day.

GTA 6 is set for release on Thursday, Nov. 19. Don't forget to submit your PTO request.

The game was announced in December 2023 and was originally supposed to launch in late 2025, but Rockstar delayed it to May 2026 and then again to this fall. Both times, the company said more time was needed in order to deliver "the level of quality" and "polish" that players "expect and deserve." Hey, what's a couple more months after 13 years?

Rockstar told IGN that GTA 6 will be "a single-player experience," but it seems likely that the game will eventually get a multiplayer mode given the massive success of GTA Online. (That's the multiplayer side of 2013's GTA 5, the most recent installment of the Grand Theft Auto franchise.) It could come in the form of a free update or a separate purchase; Rockstar added GTA Online to GTA 5 two weeks after its release, then made it available as a $20 standalone title in 2022.

Twitch CEO Dan Clancy told Bloomberg in September that he expects Rockstar to add multiplayer to GTA 6 next year, but Rockstar itself has yet to confirm that.

GTA 6 is reportedly launching on PC in early 2027, according to the gaming business site GamesBeat.

#319. How to get this Siri camera upgrade for your iPad before its public release

You’ll be able to access Visual Intelligence from a new tab in the tablet's Camera app.

Apple users willing to install beta versions of its OS updates can get their hands on upcoming features sooner than most, as long as they're comfortable contending with the possibility of buggy software or apps breaking. And one such feature is the Siri Mode that's already available in the camera app on supported iPhones. It's also coming to compatible iPads as part of iPadOS 27.3 and will let you ask Siri AI questions about things you point your tablet's camera at.

We got our first look at this mode when Apple properly unveiled Siri AI at its Worldwide Developers Conference in June this year. It basically brings the company's Visual Intelligence tool for asking questions from photos to the camera app. But there's one distinguishing feature that's unique to iPads which isn't available on iPhones.

On iPads, Visual Intelligence includes Apple Pencil support. So, much like with Google's Circle to Search feature, you can snap a photo of something with your iPad, use your Apple Pencil to draw a circle around an item and learn more information about the object with the help of Siri AI.

It's worth noting that Visual Intelligence is already available on compatible tablets — iPad Pro and iPad Air with the M1 chip and newer, and iPad mini with the A17 Pro or A20 Pro chipset. As things stand, you can access it in the Camera app by long-pressing the Camera Control button or pressing the Action button if you've set it up to activate the feature (and your iPad has an Action button, of course).

In iPadOS 27.3, you'll have another way to access Visual Intelligence in the Camera app. All you'll have to do is swipe through the options in the viewfinder (i.e. photo, video and portrait modes) until you get to the Siri tab.

The company is expected to release the public version of iPadOS 27.3 in December. However, you can try it on your tablet right now, because Apple released the first beta in early November.

To install the iPadOS 27.3 beta, open the Settings app on your iPad, then tap General > Software Update. If you haven't already done so, you'll need to enable beta updates. Tap the Beta Updates option, then iPadOS 27 Public Beta. After you go back to the Software Update tab, you should be able to install the iPadOS 27.3 beta.

Before you install the beta, it's best to create a full backup of your iPad using iCloud, a Mac or a Windows PC. Beta builds aren't the finished product, meaning they're more susceptible to bugs and errors. Backing up your data should be standard practice anyway, but it's especially advisable to do that before installing a beta version of an operating system, just to make sure your files and other information stay safe.

If you don't feel comfortable enough to install a iPadOS beta, that's perfectly fine! The public version of the OS update is likely coming soon. You can check out the Siri tab on your iPad's Camera app at that point.

#317. Free video-streaming service Tubi lets users share curated watchlists now

User-based recommendations could make it easier to find stuff to watch on Tubi.

Tubi users can now create and share playlists of movies and shows available on the streaming service.

Tubi is a free ad-supported streaming television (FAST) service that has both live channels and on-demand content.

Today, the Fox-owned company announced “Mixtapes” as a way for users to make watchlists from Tubi’s “nearly 400,000 movies and TV episodes,” Tubi’s announcement said, adding:

They can give each mixtape a title and description and arrange the entertainment inside in whatever order feels right to them. Viewers can build one entirely on their own, or describe what they’re looking for and let Tubi offer a starting place that they can edit, rearrange, or rebuild entirely. When their mixtape is finished, they can publish and share it with other people or keep it for themselves.

Tubi’s Mixtapes website already features numerous playlists, including: “Millennial Memory Lane”—with movies like Cruel Intentions (1999) and The Fifth Element (1997) and shows such as Everybody Hates Chris (2005–2009) and Degrassi: The Next Generation (2001–2015); and “Bald Baddies”—which includes Fast & Furious Presents: Hobbs & Shaw (2019) and Central Intelligence (2016).

Watching a Mixtape doesn’t require a Tubi account, but making one does.

Tubi’s Mixtapes comes at a time when streaming service providers are seeking a way to address the content fragmentation that’s frequently cited as a frustration among streaming users, as well as users struggling to find something to watch. Gracenote’s “2025 State of Play” report, for instance, surveyed 3,000 consumers in the US, Brazil, France, Germany, Mexico, and the UK and found that “nearly 33 percent of streaming users feel content and service fragmentation negatively impacts their TV experience.”

Users are also likely to stop using a streaming service if they struggle to find something they want to watch, per the survey. Forty-nine percent of users said they “are willing to cancel a service based on difficulty finding what to watch.” Additionally, 19 percent of respondents said they would “abandon a viewing session if their content search is not successful.”

Although Tubi users don’t have paid subscriptions to cancel, making it easier to find relevant content could help Tubi grow its user base and increase the time people spend watching, which supports the advertising business Tubi relies on.

Sharable watchlists are rare among streaming services. Mubi has a similar feature where users can make lists of movies and shows that people can follow, but the lists can feature content that’s not available on Mubi.

Plex and JustWatch also let people create and share curated watchlists with other users.

Still, it’s nice to see a streaming service, especially a free one, add new capabilities at a time when many other streaming services are consistently increasing subscription prices. And while some streaming service companies are turning to AI to help with content recommendations, a human connection may appeal more to certain users, while keeping costs lower for Tubi.

#316. The haunting Masters meltdown that changed Rory McIlroy's career

Editor’s Note:  This story was originally published in April 2023.

Slumped on his club, head buried in his arm, Rory McIlroy looked on the verge of tears.

The then-21-year-old had just watched his ball sink into the waters of Rae’s Creek at Augusta National and with it, his dream of winning The Masters, a dream that had looked so tantalizingly close mere hours earlier.

As a four-time major winner and one of the most decorated names in the sport’s history, few players would turn down the chance to swap places with McIlroy heading into Augusta this week.

Yet on Sunday afternoon of April 10, 2011, not a golfer in the world would have wished to be in the Northern Irishman’s shoes.

A fresh-faced, mop-headed McIlroy had touched down in Georgia for the first major of the season with a reputation as the leading light of the next generation of stars.

An excellent 2010 had marked his best season since turning pro three years earlier, highlighted by a first PGA Tour win at the Quail Hollow Championship and a crucial contribution to Team Europe’s triumph at the Ryder Cup.

Yet despite a pair of impressive top-three finishes at the Open and PGA Championship respectively, a disappointing missed cut at The Masters – his first at a major – served as ominous foreshadowing.

McIlroy shot 74 and 77 to fall four strokes short of the cut line at seven-over par, a performance that concerned him enough to take a brief sabbatical from competition.

But one year on in 2011, any lingering Masters demons looked to have been exorcised as McIlroy flew round the Augusta fairways.

Having opened with a bogey-free seven-under 65 – the first time he had ever shot in the 60s at the major – McIlroy pulled ahead from Spanish first round co-leader Alvaro Quirós with a second round 69.

It sent him into the weekend holding a two-shot cushion over Australia’s Jason Day, with Tiger Woods a further stroke behind and back in the hunt for a 15th major after a surging second round 66.

And yet the 21-year-old leader looked perfectly at ease with having a target on his back. Even after a tentative start to the third round, McIlroy rallied with three birdies across the closing six holes to stretch his lead to four strokes heading into Sunday.

The youngster was out on his own ahead of a bunched chasing pack comprising Day, Ángel Cabrera, K.J. Choi and Charl Schwartzel. After 54 holes, McIlroy had shot just three bogeys.

“It’s a great position to be in … I’m finally feeling comfortable on this golf course,” McIlroy told reporters.

“I’m not getting ahead of myself, I know how leads can dwindle away very quickly. I have to go out there, not take anything for granted and go out and play as hard as I’ve played the last three days. If I can do that, hopefully things will go my way.

“We’ll see what happens tomorrow because four shots on this golf course isn’t that much.”

The truth can hurt, and McIlroy was about to prove his assessment of Augusta to be true in the most excruciating way imaginable.

His fourth bogey of the week arrived immediately. Having admitted to expecting some nerves at the first tee, McIlroy sparked a booming opening drive down the fairway, only to miss his putt from five feet.

Three consecutive pars steadied the ship, but Schwartzel had the wind in his sails. A blistering birdie, par, eagle start had seen him draw level at the summit after his third hole.

A subsequent bogey from the South African slowed his charge, as McIlroy clung onto a one-shot lead at the turn from Schwartzel, Cabrera, Choi, and a rampaging Woods, who shot five birdies and an eagle across the front nine to send Augusta into a frenzy.

Despite his dwindling advantage and the raucous Tiger-mania din ahead of him, McIlroy had responded well to another bogey at the 5th hole, draining a brilliant 20-foot putt at the 7th to restore his lead.

The fist pump that followed marked the high-water point of McIlroy’s round, as a sliding start accelerated into full-blown free-fall at the par-four 10th hole.

His tee shot went careening into a tree, ricocheting to settle between the white cabins that separate the main course from the adjacent par-three course. It offered viewers a glimpse at a part of Augusta rarely seen on broadcast, followed by pictures of McIlroy anxiously peering out from behind a tree to track his follow-up shot.

Though his initial escape was successful, yet another collision with a tree and a two-putt on the green saw a stunned McIlroy eventually tap in for a triple bogey. Having led the field one hole and seven shots earlier, he arrived at the 11th tee in seventh.

By the time his tee drive at the 13th plopped into the creek, all thoughts of who might be the recipient of the green jacket had long-since switched away from the anguished youngster. It had taken him seven putts to navigate the previous two greens, as a bogey and a double bogey dropped him to five-under – the score he had held after just 11 holes of the tournament.

Mercifully, the last five holes passed without major incident. A missed putt for birdie from five feet at the final hole summed up McIlroy’s day, though he was given a rousing reception as he left the green.

Mere minutes earlier, the same crowd had erupted as Schwartzel sunk his fourth consecutive birdie to seal his first major title. After starting the day four shots adrift of McIlroy, the South African finished 10 shots ahead of him, and two ahead of second-placed Australian duo Jason Day and Adam Scott.

McIlroy’s eight-over 80 marked the highest score of the round. Having headlined the leaderboard for most of the week, he finished tied-15th.

Tears would flow during a phone call with his parents the following morning, but at his press conference, McIlroy was upbeat.

“I’m very disappointed at the minute, and I’m sure I will be for the next few days, but I’ll get over it,” he said.

“I was leading this golf tournament with nine holes to go, and I just unraveled … It’s a Sunday at a major, what it can do.

“This is my first experience at it, and hopefully the next time I’m in this position I’ll be able to handle it a little better. I didn’t handle it particularly well today obviously, but it was a character-building day … I’ll come out stronger for it.”

Once again, McIlroy would be proven right.

Just eight weeks later in June, McIlroy rampaged to an eight-shot victory at the US Open. Records tumbled in his wake at Congressional, as he shot a tournament record 16-under 268 to become the youngest major winner since Tiger Woods at The Masters in 1997.

The historic victory kickstarted a golden era for McIlroy. After coasting to another eight-shot win at the PGA Championship in 2012, McIlroy became only the third golfer since 1934 to win three majors by the age of 25 with triumph at the 2014 Open Championship.

Before the year was out, he would add his fourth major title with another PGA Championship win.

And much of it was owed to that fateful afternoon at Augusta. In an interview with the BBC in 2015, McIlroy dubbed it “the most important day” of his career.

“If I had not had the whole unravelling, if I had just made a couple of bogeys coming down the stretch and lost by one, I would not have learned as much.

“Luckily, it did not take me long to get into a position like that again when I was leading a major and I was able to get over the line quite comfortably. It was a huge learning curve for me and I needed it, and thankfully I have been able to move on to bigger and better things.

“Looking back on what happened in 2011, it doesn’t seem as bad when you have four majors on your mantelpiece.”

McIlroy’s contentment came with a caveat: it would be “unthinkable” if he did not win The Masters in his career.

Yet as he prepares for his 15th appearance at Augusta National this week, a green jacket remains an elusive missing item from his wardrobe.

Despite seven top-10 finishes in his past 10 Masters outings, the trophy remains the only thing separating McIlroy from joining the ranks of golf immortals to have completed golf’s career grand slam of all four majors in the modern era: Gene Sarazen, Ben Hogan, Gary Player, Jack Nicklaus, and Tiger Woods.

A runner-up finish to Scottie Scheffler last year marked McIlroy’s best finish at Augusta, yet arguably 2011 remains the closest he has ever been to victory. A slow start in 2022 meant McIlroy had begun Sunday’s deciding round 10 shots adrift of the American, who teed off for his final hole with a five-shot lead despite McIlroy’s brilliant 64 finish.

At 33 years old, time is still on his side. Though 2022 extended his major drought to eight years, it featured arguably his best golf since that golden season in 2014.

And as McIlroy knows better than most, things can change quickly at Augusta National.

#314. iFixit teardown shows how Apple's glowing PROLED MacBook logo works

The new laptop goes the extra mile to recreate the iconic feature.

iFixit has shared its teardown of Apple's first touchscreen MacBook Pro and besides bemoaning the company's half-hearted commitment to user repairability, it also showed off the physical components that make the laptop's glowing logo work. The new lid design is one of the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display's many charms, alongside its tandem OLED display and lighter weight.

Older LCD MacBooks featured a physical cutout that let the display's backlight illuminate the logo, but since OLED screens don't require backlights, Apple had to come up with a different solution to bring the design element back. iFixit found that the company uses a light strip with four LEDs layered behind the display, whose light is reflected through a diffuser to illuminate the logo. The Apple logo itself is also coated "like a two-way mirror," according to iFixit, so that it can appear metallic and reflective when the logo is off, but still let light through when the LEDs are on.

Apple ditched the glowing logo in 2016, so it's a bit ironic that the company waited until it stopped using backlit displays to bring it back. It used to be a cool bit of branding before, but now the company may see the glowing logo design as an important way to distinguish its most expensive laptop from the kind we peons are forced to use.

The rest of the internal changes to the laptop are more expected. Apple was able to achieve the new MacBook Pro's lighter weight by using less aluminum in the base and lid of the laptop and including a slightly smaller trackpad. The laptop's hinge was also reworked to make the display wobble less when you tap the screen. Those changes don't make it any easier to fix, though. The laptop is still complex to disassemble: For example, iFixit says that you have to remove the laptop's logic board to switch out its USB-C ports. Plenty of other components, like the MacBook's memory and the HDMI port, are also soldered in place. Anyone dropping up to $15,000 on a laptop probably isn't worried about doing their own repairs, but Apple's thin and light (and glowing) design comes with some tradeoffs, as always.

#313. Netflix adds a bunch of League of Legends music videos

Netflix and Riot have teamed up to capture the attention of League of Legends fans, adding a host of music videos to the platform.

Ahead of Worlds (the League of Legends World Championship) taking over New York's Barclays Center this weekend, the streaming platform is adding a collection of videos from Riot's virtual artists, including K/DA, True Damage, and HEARTSTEEL.

A continued collaboration between Riot and Netflix, the League of Legends hub will go live Nov. 12 and will include music videos animated by Fortiche, the animation studio behind Netflix and Riot's critically lauded, Emmy-winning series Arcane.

You'll find six of K/DA's videos on there, including their Fortiche-made "FIRESTARTER" comeback single released in October, and True Damage's official 2026 Worlds anthem "Know My Name," produced with WIZZ, with animation direction by CRCR. Both will likely be performed at the Worlds opening ceremony on Saturday.

Of course, you can watch all these videos on YouTube, Netflix's longtime rival.

It seems Netflix is leaning into custom content hubs for specific titles and partnerships of late, with short-form videos the focus. The news comes days after Netflix added a KPop Demon Hunters hub to the platform, collating the film, a sing-a-long version, lyric videos for songs including "Golden," HUNTR/X choreography videos, featurettes, and more.

#312. Why your Roku might be plugged into the wrong HDMI port

Your Roku might lose a few features if you plug it into any old HDMI port on your TV.

Depending on your smart TV, an add-on media streaming device isn't necessarily essential, but they can still provide a lot of value. Perhaps your TV's processor can't keep up with the latest apps and features, or you might prefer a specific ecosystem. That's what Apple is banking on to sell the 2026 Apple TV 4K, which is now an extremely pricey box at $299.

Roku streamers are far cheaper, but can still come with other concerns. You might be worried about your Roku monitoring what you watch, for example, or the implications of Fox's pending takeover. On a more mundane level, there's an issue applicable to any connected device: getting the best possible performance from your TV.

To do that, it's vital to you select the right HDMI port and cable. It's hard to go too far wrong with Roku devices, but a mistake could still mean getting inferior video or sound.

The reason it's hard to go wrong is that for the most part, Roku devices don't exploit any features unique to HDMI 2.1 or later, and all modern TVs support at least HDMI 2.0. Standards like 4K, HDR10+, Dolby Atmos and Dolby Vision can all work over 2.0, if not necessarily in their optimal form. Optimal would include 120Hz-plus refresh rates — but even Roku's top-of-the-line Ultra is capped at 60Hz, regardless of which port you plug it into.

That's acceptable, since 24fps is the most common playback speed for TV and movies while 30fps is used for news. 60fps content (equaling 60Hz) is common on YouTube, Twitch and many live TV streaming services; any modern Roku will support this. 120Hz would simply enable clearer motion, which is mostly relevant for gaming.

The Roku Ultra does support an HDMI 2.1 feature called Quick Media Switching (QMS). This allows refresh rates to shift up or down seamlessly, like if you're flipping from the 30fps evening news to the 24fps cinematic standard. Without QMS, you're going to run into temporary blackouts when a frame rate shift occurs.

In some cases, only certain HDMI ports on your TV will support HDR standards like Dolby Vision. Or if that barrier doesn't exist, you might have to go into your TV's input settings to flip a port over to the appropriate "enhanced" option. Ports are often set to reduced capabilities by default for the sake of compatibility. But since it's unlikely that you'd run into conflicts, you might as well enable modern functionality across the board. Check your manufacturer's website to figure out which ports support HDR and which menus you might need to navigate.

Another important point here is that you shouldn't plug a Roku into your TV's ARC or eARC port. While that technology is essential for some audio features like Dolby Atmos, labeled ports are intended for compatible output devices like soundbars, receivers and other speaker systems. If a TV supports ARC or eARC, it's automatically pulling audio from every HDMI 2.x input, regardless of how it's marked.

Along with the right ports, it's also important to choose a matching cable. If your Roku does support HDMI 2.1 features, a 2.0 cable will prevent them from working. If there's any doubt about what you have, HDMI 2.1 and even 2.2 (also known as Ultra96, which is not in wide use yet) cables are easy to find at low prices.

Computers and game consoles should always get priority for available HDMI 2.1 or 2.2 ports, since they can fully take advantage of high refresh rates and VRR (variable refresh rate). Streamers like Rokus are further back in the line, at least until manufacturers start taking advantage of everything newer TVs are capable of.

#310. Pixel phones getting new icon styles, Create My Widget, and more in November Pixel Drop

Spruce up your home screen with expanded custom icons and AI-generated widgets.

Google’s Pixel phones get an update every month, but most are just security patches and bug fixes. The bigger changes are bundled into quarterly Pixel Drops, and one is rolling out today. The update brings a few new things, and many of the Gemini features previously exclusive to the latest Pixel phones will also expand to Google’s Pixel 10 lineup.

The Pixel 11 was Google’s first foray into “Gemini Intelligence” branding, something we’ll see a lot more of going forward. Some of the features under that heading were limited to Google’s newest phones (and in some cases, Samsung’s phones). The November Pixel Drop will expand these AI features to the Pixel 10 lineup, but importantly, that doesn’t include the budget-oriented Pixel 10a.

For starters, the Pixel 10 series will get the improved personalized suggestions (previously known as Magic Cue) that debuted on the Pixel 11. This enables things like sending grocery lists to Google Keep from other apps and generating summaries from the context menu. Rambler, Google’s AI-assisted voice input system, is also coming to the 2025 phones. Likewise, Gemini app automation will roll out on Pixel 10 with this update. This feature is still limited to select apps in rideshare, food delivery, travel, and a few other categories.

The AI-powered icon theming that Google added to Pixels back in March is also getting an update. Initially, this feature just matched icon colors to the system theme when developers didn’t include the necessary theming assets. With this update, Google says it has moved the feature to a more powerful Nano Banana model, unlocking 10 new icon styles for your home screen.

The update also brings Create My Widget to Pixel phones. Google announced this earlier in 2026, and it shipped on Googlebooks a few weeks ago. The idea is that you describe a home screen widget you want, and Android builds it for you on the spot. However, the use cases are pretty narrow. You’re limited to data accessible in search (e.g., stock prices, sports scores, and weather) and select Google account data points like Gmail or your calendar.

So that’s a lot of AI. Google does have some non-AI additions with this update, but don’t get your hopes up. One of the headlining additions is (drumroll) HiLight support for Google Messages. Yes, Pixel 11 phones will be able to illuminate HiLight when you get a message from important contacts. Ignoring that we had more functional notification LEDs 20 years ago, this is a good and necessary improvement for Google’s least useful hardware feature.

If you have a Pixel Watch, Google says this update will also improve your workout routine. The Pixel Watch 3 and newer will be able to display step-by-step guidance for your workouts. The wearable can also log reps and weights as you go without interrupting live metrics. While Create My Widget is not yet available on the watch, Google promises it’s also coming soon.

The update is beginning its rollout today and should be available widely in the coming days.

#308. Teenage swimmer breaks two world records in four days

McIntosh finished the 400m individual medley in a time of 4:25.87, breaking the mark set at the 2016 Rio Olympics by Hungarian swimmer Katinka Hosszú with a time of 4:26.36.

The Canadian teen made her Olympic debut aged 14 at Tokyo 2020, where she finished fourth in the 200-meter freestyle.

She bagged gold in the 200m butterfly and 400m medley at the world championships in 2022, as well as two golds at the Commonwealth Games in the 200m and 400m medleys.

She now trains in Florida under coach Brent Arckey with the Sarasota Sharks team.

#307. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 11, 2026

The Moon is now a little further into its Waxing Crescent phase, with a small amount of its surface illuminated by the Sun. It should be slightly easier to spot tonight, although there's still not much to see in the way of lunar features.

As of Wednesday, Nov. 11, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waxing Crescent phase, with 4% of its surface visible.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

The Moon takes approximately 29.5 days to complete one full cycle. As it orbits Earth, the amount of sunlight reflecting off its surface that we can see changes, creating the different phases. This takes us from the New Moon to the Full Moon and back again, before the cycle begins once more, according to NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

#306. What is the average lifespan of recordable Blu-ray discs?

They can last you quite a while, but there are several factors to consider.

At this point, Blu-ray is a niche format, having mostly been supplanted by downloads, streaming and flash storage. But it's easily the format of choice for cinephiles, owing to higher ceilings on picture and audio quality. And if you want to archive files for a long time, Blu-ray may be more affordable, what with flash memory prices spiraling out of control.

How much can you trust recordable Blu-ray discs (BD-R), though? It's not an academic question if the files you're stashing are irreplaceable. If you make a bad decision, you could potentially lose your only digital copies of home videos, say, or a history of all your artistic projects.

Generally speaking, the news here is positive. BD-R may legitimately be your best option. There can be mitigating factors though, such as manufacturing quality, or simply having something to access the content on several years down the road.

Short of accidents, the main threat to Blu-ray discs is something called disc rot. This is actually an umbrella term for various forms of decay. The reflective layer can gradually become oxidized, for example. Moisture might attract mold, or cause the layers of a disc to separate. Then there's the matter of exposure to UV (ultraviolet) light, though that's more likely to cause labeling to fade than harm your data. You can potentially notice rot before popping in a disc — look for signs like discoloration or spots.

According to Canadian Conservation Institute data, the cheapest BD-R discs will last five to 10 years on average. These should be avoided for any kind of archival project, since an external SSD is liable to last at least that long, and possibly several times as much while storing a lot more data. Remember, even the biggest BD-R discs top out at 128GB.

That said, manufacturing quality can have a huge impact. In better BD-Rs, a dye recording layer is replaced by a silicon and copper alloy, which you can tell if a disc appears dark gray or grayish-brown. Likewise, these discs might switch to a gold metal layer. The result, the CCI says, is double the lifespan at 10 to 20 years.

It gets better. BD-RE (rewritable) discs can conceivably last you as much as 20 to 50 years. That's so long that you might be able to pass them down to your children or grandchildren. On those timescales, however, other factors should probably discourage you from relying on Blu-ray.

The most obvious reason is that discs are fragile. While storage in a relatively dry, dark and cool environment can deter rot, it's all too easy to accidentally crack or scratch a disc in a way that leaves it unreadable. Conversely, many external SSDs use tough metal enclosures that make physical destruction unlikely. All it might take to ruin your disc collection is forgetting that it's at the bottom of a moving box you're hauling, not the top.

Ultimately, the bigger issue might be industry support for Blu-ray. Optical drives have vanished as a default feature on computers, and companies are slowly pulling out the market for both external burners and BD-R discs, even Sony. At the moment, it's not that difficult to find burners and discs if you shop online. But there's no telling whether anyone will still be making this hardware 10 years down the road, much less 20. You could end up with an immaculately-preserved collection and no way of accessing it. Even your current drive might become useless if its software drivers cease to be compatible with later operating systems.

Whatever route you take, it's best not to be completely reliant on one storage medium for critical files. The general rule of thumb these days is to keep one copy in the cloud, and two others on separate forms of local storage. That's not always realistic — at the least, however, you should always be in a position to migrate your files to something fresher.

#304. Google Fi adds support for a second 5G network, won't say which one

Fi has always supported T-Mobile, but the new one? Google won’t say.

Google has announced a “super network” upgrade for its Fi cell service. For the past few years, Google Fi has operated exclusively on the T-Mobile network, but now it’s adding support for a second 5G network. Oddly, Google refuses to say which one.

Google’s MVNO (mobile virtual network operator) launched in 2015 as Project Fi with support for both T-Mobile and Sprint service. The service initially only worked on Google’s Nexus phones, which would switch between networks based on signal strength. After T-Mobile absorbed Sprint, Fi switched to being a single-network service in the US while continuing to hop between networks for international travel.

With the super network, Google Fi is again a dual-carrier service stateside. Phones connected to Fi will be able to hop between T-Mobile 5G and a different 5G network to provide the best possible connectivity. Google’s blog post announcing the change conspicuously doesn’t mention either network, saying only that it supports “two of America’s best 5G cellular networks.”

We know one of them is T-Mobile, and the other must be either AT&T or Verizon. When reached for comment, a Google spokesperson declined to name the second network, which is odd. Looking at the Fi coverage map, my best guess for the super network partner is Verizon. The shape of unserved regions is a closer match than AT&T, but carriers do tend to fudge the fine details of these maps.

MVNOs don’t always advertise which network they use, and that information is getting harder to find in FAQs and support pages. Google’s Fi FAQ no longer even confirms T-Mobile as a network partner. Google used to be very upfront about its partnership with T-Mobile, pointing out that, unlike most MVNOs, it gets top prioritization on T-Mobile’s towers. Network information like this is really something consumers should have when shopping for plans—you probably know which carriers work best where you spend your time.

There are a few caveats in the super network rollout beyond the mysterious new partner. Currently, network switching is only supported on the Pixel 9, Pixel 10, and Pixel 11 series. Other devices will get access in 2027. You also won’t be able to enjoy traditional dual-SIM functionality if you want Fi to switch networks. This will only work if you have Fi configured as an eSIM with all non-Fi SIMs disabled.

If you meet those requirements, super network support is rolling out now at no additional cost on Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans.

#302. Samsung Gallery now syncs with Google to back up your photos

The company ended Gallery's Microsoft OneDrive integration back in September.

If you've ever owned a Samsung phone, you've most likely seen your device prompt you to back up your media to OneDrive at least once in the past. Back in September, however, Samsung removed its Gallery app's ability to sync with Microsoft OneDrive. Well, the phonemaker was likely cooking up another partnership behind the scenes, because it has just announced that its Gallery app can now sync with your Google account.

In the past, if you wanted to back up your photos and videos to your Google storage on a Samsung phone, you've had to use Google Photos instead of (or in addition to) your device's native Gallery app. With this update, you can stick to Samsung's Gallery app if you want to.The feature is currently only available on select Galaxy devices running One UI 9, Samsung's latest custom Android interface, but the company says it will roll out to more devices in phases. To enable the feature, tap the Cloud icon in Gallery or go to Gallery Settings and switch on Sync photos and videos.

Since the feature syncs media files to your Google account, you'll be able to access them in the Gallery app of another Samsung Galaxy device. Simply log into the same account on your other device and enable sync on it, as well. You'll then be able to see previews of your synced photos and videos on that device. If you want to access the original files, just select and download them. In case you want to do the opposite and remove files from your phone or tablet after they've been synced to Google storage, you can use Samsung's other new feature. It offers a Free up space option in the Sync photos and videos settings, which deletes original files from your device while keeping thumbnails visible in the Gallery app.

It's worth noting that Gallery sync will take up storage space on your Google account. Each Google account comes with 5GB of free storage by default, which you can expand to 15GB if you verify a valid phone number. If you want or need more than that, since that storage is shared across Google's services, you'll have to purchase a Google One subscription that will set you back $2 per month for 100GB of storage or $3 for 200GB.

#299. What happens to mathematics when AI becomes an oracle?

Mathematician Steven Strogatz discusses his new book, Big Math: The Hidden Codes That Run Our World.

Our modern world runs on math, from social media algorithms, package delivery, and weather prediction to medical imaging and machine learning. The rise of computers helped fuel its dominance, enabling us to work with increasingly large numbers that would otherwise be beyond the capacity of our finite human brains. But with the rise of large language models, AI chatbots, and, soon, quantum computing, so-called “big math” is beginning to outstrip its creators entirely. What that means, and how we might adapt, is the focus of a new book, Big Math: The Hidden Codes That Run Our World, by applied mathematicians Steven Strogatz and Alex Townsend.

Strogatz is perhaps best known in his field for his co-authorship of a seminal 1998 paper with Duncan Watts on the collective dynamics of small-world networks. He has also studied chaos theory as applied to physics, the geometry of supercoiled DNA, and the biological collective oscillators, such as swarms of synchronously flashing fireflies and crickets that chirp in unison. In 2005, Strogatz extended his study of synchronicity to the crowds that caused the wobbling of London’s Millennium Bridge.

An award-winning communicator, Strogatz wrote three series of columns on mathematics for The New York Times in 2010, 2012, and 2025 and hosted a podcast for Quanta magazine from 2020–2021. He has written four popular books: Sync (2003), The Calculus of Friendship (2009), The Joy of X (2012), and the New York Times bestseller Infinite Powers (2019).

This time around, Strogatz has found a well-matched co-author in Townsend, a younger colleague recently hired at Cornell who studies numerical analysis and deep learning. As senior faculty, Strogatz was assigned to mentor Townsend, and the two men began regularly lunching together in Risley Hall. Strogatz was intrigued by the popular book potential of Townsend’s course on the top 20 algorithms of the 20th century and suggested co-authorship. That concept slowly morphed into a book about big matrix calculations, and then into what became Big Math.

“There are so many books about pure math, as if computers didn’t exist,” Strogatz told Ars. “Alex and I are both very applied mathematicians. We thought there should be a book on computers and math, especially the class of really big problems with billions of variables or parameters that you just can’t approach in your head. A lot of the math we’ve had for centuries perfectly scales up to this computer moment. What the abacus did for arithmetic, the fangcheng (“rectangular array”) board did for systems of linear equations. It was a matrix you could manipulate with your hand and bamboo sticks. So this externalizing of math outside of our heads into our tools is a 2,000-year-old idea.”

Mathematics now faces its greatest challenge yet in the age of AI, sparking heated debates on the role of human reasoning when the machines are making most of the major breakthroughs. “The mathematician in each of us will always want to know more,” Strogatz and Townsend wrote. “The human being must insist that knowledge serves something larger than itself. That might be the work of our century: letting our power grow without losing our soul. Big Math keeps getting bigger. So must we.”

Ars caught up with Strogatz to learn more.

Ars Technica: Let’s start by defining “big math,” because it’s such a broad umbrella term.

Steven Strogatz: Operationally, big math is “big” relative to human cognitive capacity. So it can be surprisingly small and yet big. We also wanted to expand it to things that people would think of as computational engineering—in the building of the Burj Khalifa, that’d be civil engineering or design architecture. It’s there in medical imaging. It’s in getting us to the Moon. The lunar module was controlled by a tiny computer that only had to think about 20 or 30 variables at a time to steer the LEM down to the Moon’s surface. But it’s not something a person could do in their head.

Big Math is not simply big in the ordinary sense of big numbers. There’s an undercurrent in the book about the importance of linearity and sparsity. When problems are linear, they can be scaled up to immense size, and they stay tractable. If they’re non-linear, you don’t have any hope of dealing with them with pure math. You can do them with computers. But for the people who want rigorous theorems, we’re in terra incognita when we deal with anything that’s non-linear and big.

Small math can be very hard. Historically, we had small math problems with one or two variables, arithmetic, algebra that you learn in high school. Even to this day, a lot of pure math is relatively small. It doesn’t concern itself with enormous numbers of variables simultaneously because it’s totally intractable. Cryptography can be small, but it’s so complicated with its non-linearities and other algebraic difficulties. So big is one way to be difficult, but there are a lot of other ways.

Nowadays, trillions of parameters are typical in the biggest of the large language models. But infinity is not big from this point. Infinity, which you would think of would be the biggest of big math, is not because calculus fits perfectly nicely in a human head. Infinity turns out to be very tractable in a way that a billion is not.

Ars: One of your real-world examples of big math is particularly relevant at the moment: mathematics as it relates to the gerrymandering issue.

Strogatz: It’s an interesting question of, what’s a fair map and what’s a reasonable redistricting? The legal system gives us certain criteria, but they are vague. The districts should not be too snakey-looking, not look like salamanders, for instance. The term in the law is ‘compact.’ They should be reasonably compact. But what does that mean mathematically? The districts are also supposed to be contiguous; you don’t want to have separate islands and call that one district. It’s supposed to respect natural boundaries like rivers or other landscape features. Also, each district is supposed to have roughly the same population.

So how to make maps that respect all of the criteria? It turns out there are a lot of ways to make them. Using past voting information, you can stack the deck. That’s what gerrymandering is all about, trying to redistrict in favor of whatever party is in power. There has been a big debate about whether certain maps violate people’s rights. The principle should be one person, one vote. If you do things that dilute somebody’s power so that their votes are wasted, so to speak, that’s traditionally thought to be unfair or maybe even illegal.

The Supreme Court has heard all kinds of cases, but the court is very resistant to the use of math. I quoted [Chief Justice] John Roberts talking about the efficiency gap as one measure of gerrymandering. He referred to it by its Greek letters and said something like, “The average person on the street is just going to think this is gobbledygook, that this is not a criterion that the law should pay any attention to.”

Nevertheless, Moon Duchin and her colleagues showed this very down-to-earth way of generating a big ensemble of reasonable maps that satisfy all the legal criteria so that you have a baseline, you could do the control experiment. What the math does is give you a principled way of generating maps at random that respect the criteria that the law requires, but otherwise are just a random sample of reasonable maps. How often would a map produce the result that the gerrymander intended? If you can show that it’s an extreme outlier, that’s a measure of something. Does it prove malicious intent? Not really. It does prove it’s not typical.

There was an Alabama voting rights case where Roberts actually voted with the plaintiffs, who said that there was real harm being done to Black voters in Alabama with one of Alabama’s maps. The state was claiming there’s no way we could get a reasonable map to have two districts with Black majority voters. Moon Duchin showed that was wrong. You could generate thousands of maps that would do that. So it just wasn’t true what the state had claimed.

After the book was copyedited and ready to go to press, a Supreme Court decision was made that, in fact, you can partisan gerrymander as much as you want. It doesn’t violate the Voting Rights Act. We wrote a more optimistic story.

Ars: How has math changed over time from a philosophical standpoint?

Strogatz: I think a crisp way to encapsulate it is the role of the computer. For a long time, a computer was a servant to mathematicians and engineers and scientists. It was no more philosophically threatening than a microscope or a telescope. It could extend our senses. It’s a chisel, it’s an anvil, it’s a tool. It helped us do calculations faster; things that would be too hard for people to do at scale you could do on a machine. We had mechanical calculators. They became electronic computers in the 1950s with the invention of the transistor and got much faster to give us today’s supercomputers. We program them; they do exactly what we tell them. So they’re servants.

The first philosophically big change is in the 1950s, when computers start to be used as more than servants; they become scouts. You could send the computer out into unknown, unexplored territory in the mathematical landscape. The clearest example was work by Enrico Fermi and his colleagues (Pasta, Ulam, and Tsingou) that we wrote about in the chapter on simulation. Mary Tsingou was the one who did the calculations in what was traditionally called the Fermi-Pasta-Ulam numerical experiment.

What distinguished it from earlier uses of computers is that Fermi and colleagues had no idea what the answer was going to be. It’s one thing to use a computer to calculate the power released by an atomic bomb blast or to calculate an artillery shell’s trajectory. We didn’t know the exact numerical values, but we knew what was happening. With this experiment, they were trying to simulate a simplified model of how atoms could, in the jargon, formalize as chemical bonds jiggle. You want to see the decay to entropy. How do systems degenerate to greater and greater disorder if they’re just left to their own devices?

Fermi thought that the non-linear aspect of chemical bonds—meaning if you’d stretch a chemical bond by twice as much, it doesn’t pull back exactly twice as hard, so it’s not strictly proportional—might account for the so-called second law of thermodynamics, the decay to entropy. Pencil and paper math was not up to the job because you couldn’t calculate these non-linear interactions far enough to see the decay into entropy.

So they ran it in the computer, and a shocking thing happened: It didn’t decay. It showed a strange echo. You started all the energy in one of the modes of vibration of this system, and then it started to degenerate. But after a while, 98 percent of the energy came back into the original mode. That was the echo that they called a recurrence phenomenon. It led us to a whole new branch of math that we call soliton theory. It was an early indication that once you walk into the nonlinear jungle, human intuition breaks down. We couldn’t walk there, but we sent our scout, this computer probe, into the wilderness of the mind, and it was the only thing capable of doing the calculations and reporting back to us what it found.

Ars: The rapid rise of AI in mathematics has alarmed many in the field, particularly in light of OpenAI’s release last month of 722 AI-generated math papers, dubbed the “mathocalypse.” Technically, AI is also a new tool, but it seems fundamentally different.

Strogatz: So from servant to scout was a new thing, and computing was our partner. At first, it was all very thrilling. It was like having a new Ferrari or something. You could race ahead and then discover things. That’s what Fermi and Ulam said at the time. That was truly amazing. They didn’t know what to expect. The computer was now teaching us by showing us things, but we could still keep up. We could develop these new theories like soliton theory, so we could rationalize what it was showing us.

What’s different now is that it’s going from servant to scout to oracle, where it’s showing us things that we don’t understand and it’s not articulate enough to explain itself. It seems to be right. A clear example would be something like Google Translate, where it’s fantastic at translating one language into another, but it has not discovered new principles of linguistics, or if it has, it can’t tell us what they are.

Likewise, protein folding with AlphaFold that won the Nobel Prize in chemistry a couple of years ago for the people who built it. We now can solve a thousand times more proteins than we had before, but we have not done it by learning new physics or chemistry. It just recognized patterns, but it can’t tell us how it’s doing it. So that’s where we are, and it’s going to get worse.

Ars: In your book, you ask if insight still matters, citing Stephen Wolfram as an example of someone who might answer that question in the negative.

Strogatz: Wolfram’s position is tricky. He pushes computational irreducibility. I love these two words, perspicuous and perspicacious. Systems can be perspicuous; you can see into them. Science has, for thousands of years, concerned itself with systems that are perspicuous, meaning you can get insight into them because you’re so perspicacious. But the question from Wolfram would be, have we been the famous drunk that’s looking under the lamppost for his keys because it’s dark everywhere else? You wouldn’t have any hope of finding them except under the lamppost.

So is it that we’ve artificially restricted ourself to systems that are transparent enough that we can develop science that our insight illuminates? I think Wolfram would claim that most of nature is computationally irreducible in the sense that there is no simple insightful theory into what’s going on. The best you can do is just run a simulation and watch what happens, similar to just letting nature unfold and watch what happens in reality.

In the jargon people use nowadays, you shouldn’t expect a compressed description, a simplified model that will account for what you’re seeing. We have seen those. Newton could explain the motion of the planets from a few simple principles and that worked, but that’s, I think in Wolfram’s take, artificially simple. Most of science is not going to end up being like that. We don’t know if Wolfram is really right about that. Wolfram, I think, is demonstrably right that many computer simulations are computationally irreducible, but nature might not be.

Ars: If you can’t see into the models, and they can’t explain what they’re doing, how do you know they’re right?

Strogatz: Let’s stipulate that we don’t understand them, and that they are not at the present able to explain themselves to us. Let’s put a little asterisk next to that part, though, because it could be that they’ll become very good teachers. Or maybe we don’t understand Google Translate or AlphaFold right now, but we will in 10 years. I do believe that there will be systems that are truly opaque because of human limits, that we just won’t be fast enough or smart enough or have enough memory. AIs will show us something that we just won’t be able to understand even with their help. What happens then?

Then I think math starts to be more like science or medicine, or engineering. At the FDA, for a new drug to be approved, it has to be shown to be safe by doing a lot of controlled studies and randomized trials. There’s a whole methodology for opacity that people in medicine have worked out. But the doctors do not require knowledge of the mechanism of action. You don’t have to know how aspirin works. You don’t have to know how electroconvulsive therapy works for people with intractable depression. Even if we don’t understand, we can still live with these technologies or drugs by these methods. You don’t have to have first principles understanding. If the AI is an oracle, you can still check it by seeing how often it is right.

Strogatz: It is a loss. I don’t want to pooh-pooh the severity of the—to use a fancy philosophical word—epistemic loss. What we have prided ourselves on in pure math for so long is that we’re the only subject that has absolute proof. We have certainty. We’re as close to certainty as anything that humanity can do. We can write a proof. Euclid’s proofs are still correct today, even though he wrote them in 300 BC. There’s nothing in the science or medicine of 300 BC that is still accepted, but all of the math is still true.

So there is something eternal and very special about math. Even though we’re just primates, somehow we’re tapping into something really deep. And so to say, “Well, science and engineering are the models for math,” would be a big sacrifice to the purest of pure mathematicians. They don’t want to give up the certainty that we’ve always been so proud of.

Ars: There’s a sadness among many mathematicians, not just for the epistemic questions, but also for the impact on why we do math in the first place. You write about that “aha!” moment when you’re struggling with a problem and suddenly you see the pattern. Are we losing that?

Strogatz: There’s so many things that mathematicians are in grief about, those of us who feel grief. I have been through the whole sequence of the five stages of grief. I’m joking with you a little. But the young students don’t think this is funny. I’m an old person at the end of my career, so it’s one thing for me to be philosophical about it. But imagine being 25 years old and you love this subject and you’ve dreamt about being a mathematician, and now people are saying the subject is over. There are even people gloating about it. Some of the people in the tech community are very cruel about what’s happening to math.

We talked about the epistemic aspect, but there’s also the culture of productive struggle. The pure math people like Terry Tao have been emphasizing understanding. That’s our idea of insight, that when you try hard to understand something, even if you don’t solve it, you often are so resourceful in thinking of strategies or other simplified versions of the problem. You learn a lot on the journey. You also have all this companionship and human joy of being with other people who love the thing you love. This is not restricted to math. The people who love art or weaving—my colleagues in creative writing are bereft over what their students are experiencing now, handing in AI [generated work]. They want writing with soul, with human individuality.

Ars: That is the opposite of what AI does. I guess I worry that it will do the same thing to math. There is a beauty to math, an aesthetic element. If all you’re focused on is getting the answers and ignore the journey, those are qualitative losses. Mathematicians have individual voices, too.

Strogatz: Totally. There are people whose style is very visible in the questions they ask, in the approach they take to them, in the way they explain themselves. I have cried on camera for several interviews over the past few months because there are lots of things maybe being lost. But I want to put an underline on the fact that we don’t really know. For example, what if AI gets really good at teaching us? I find when I interact with it, that it can be a very effective explainer.

And why do we have to feel sad? Maybe we can understand math better than ever with the help of this machine. We may not get to make the greatest discoveries because the machine will probably be making them, but we can still make little discoveries. I never made a great discovery. I’m not at the level of a Fields medalist, but I can still enjoy math and so can people way below me. There are recreational mathematicians who take pleasure in discovery, whatever they figure out on their own. Just like when a person solves a puzzle, it’s not like a jigsaw puzzle or Sudoku is important. You just have the pleasure of thinking.

There are people who love the workout part of it. There are people who like the competition. We shouldn’t pretend. There are people who are thrilled by the idea of being the first to make a big discovery. That’s animating for some folks, and it will be hard for them.

Strogatz: So far the AIs are not good at asking questions. I think they could be trained to ask good questions. A graduate student is often not good at asking questions when they start because in college you’re not taught to ask questions, you’re taught to give answers. The hardest thing in training a new PhD or postdoc is helping them figure out how to ask questions. I think we could do that with AIs, too.

There is a feeling of loss, but I don’t think we should shortchange the feeling of excitement. There’s so many practical, important benefits that could happen in energy technology and climate and medicine, theoretical physics, pure math. I mean, we’re making astonishing discoveries through this machine. So for a lot of people, it’s a very joyful time. It’s very confusing. That’s the only honest assessment.

We end the book with Faust. Usually people hear “Faustian bargain” as, “You have sold your soul to the devil, now you’re going to pay.” Goethe’s version is more ambiguous: that if we keep striving to build something bigger than ourselves, maybe we can be redeemed. But it’s still a very dangerous game that we have chosen to play.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#298. Where to buy GTA 6 Limited-Edition Controller: Live restock updates at Amazon, Walmart, and Target

GTA 6 is finally arriving next week, and the heavily in-demand limited-edition PS5 DualSense controllers have been making a surprising comeback with select restocks before Rockstar’s long-awaited sequel launches.

Sony’s collaboration with Rockstar Games delivered two distinctive blue-and-purple, neon-infused designs inspired by the streets of Vice City, each available in black and white for $84.99. However, as preorders opened on Sept. 10, stock allocations sold out everywhere — especially the black version, which is sold exclusively via PlayStation Direct.

With both GTA 6 DualSense controllers scheduled for release on Nov. 19, several major stores still have preorder listings for the white edition, with intermittent restocks expected over the next week.

Be sure to keep this page bookmarked as we’ve checked across go-to retailers, confirmed where you can buy DualSense right now, and provided the full list of links you’ll need to check for the best chance of grabbing GTA 6’s limited edition controllers going forward.

Before GTA 6's launch on Nov. 19, these are the main retailers in the U.S. to bookmark and check for preorders:

PlayStation Direct (Black): Your only official U.S. retailer for the black controller. Sony has imposed a one-per-order limit, so don’t try to grab more than one if you’re lucky to get through.

PlayStation Direct (White): Also listed through Sony's storefront, the white version isn't exclusive. With that, there are several alternatives if PlayStation Direct runs out.

Amazon: A live listing for the white DualSense is available to buy now.

Walmart: The GTA 6 limited-edition PS5 DualSense controller is in stock at the time of writing. Shipping’s estimated to arrive on the game’s Nov. 19 release day.

Target: The white GTA 6 controller is in stock at the time of writing, though at the standard $84.99 price. The shipping date’s a little later than Walmart.

GameStop: Currently available to preorder online at $84.99. However, the delivery date is estimated for Nov. 20-25.

Best Buy: The white edition controller at $84.99 is out of stock online, but is currently marked as “Coming Soon."

GTA 6’s massive hype has undoubtedly led shoppers directly to the game’s dedicated PS5 DualSense controllers. It's the natural way of things.

As reported by IGN, 100 million PS5s have now been sold, including 2.1 million in the last quarter. It feels like a lot of recent sales have been driven by GTA 6. PC players need to wait, and the Xbox Series X|S’s install base pales in comparison to PlayStation, making the PS5 the default way to play GTA 6.

Not every one of the 100 million PS5 owners in the world will be playing GTA 6 or would even want the controller, but it does mean that the pool of potential buyers who’d want a dedicated GTA-themed DualSense controller is significant. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Both controllers draw inspiration from GTA 6's fictional Vice City setting, though each represents a different side of the game's Florida-inspired location.

The white DualSense adopts a brighter appearance influenced by Vice City's beaches, pastel colors, and a sunny daytime atmosphere. The black alternative draws on the city's neon-lit nightlife, with darker tones surrounding its colorful accents — representing the long nights in Vice City.

Alongside the prominent VI branding on the touchpad, each has textured palm tree detailing across the grips and a shimmering, color-shifting finish that changes appearance as the light catches it. Underneath those cosmetic changes, they're effectively standard PS5 DualSense wireless controllers. That means you're still getting haptic feedback, adaptive triggers, a built-in microphone, motion controls, and the familiar touchpad while you revel in Jason and Lucia's Leonida adventures.

Both controllers can also be used with compatible PCs, Macs, and mobile devices.

If the GTA 6 DualSense is unavailable, there are a few ways to make the search less frustrating:

Get your retailer accounts ready: Sign in to Amazon, Walmart, Target, Best Buy, and PlayStation Direct ahead of time, with your payment and delivery information saved. That way, you won't have to scramble through checkout if new stock appears.

Remember PlayStation Direct’s exclusivity for the black controller: Since it's the only official U.S. retailer carrying this version, there's little benefit in searching elsewhere unless you're considering resale listings.

Use stock alerts where available: Retailer notifications and independent trackers can help flag when inventory is returning without requiring you to manually check every product page throughout the day.

Check local stores around launch: Best Buy, Target, and GameStop could be worth contacting starting Nov. 19. In-store inventory isn't guaranteed, but it offers another option if online orders become unavailable entirely.

Avoid paying collector prices too early: Both controllers have an official $84.99 recommended retail price. Listings substantially above that are not worthwhile while there's still a chance of additional stock reaching major stores.

It's possible, although Sony hasn't confirmed a dedicated Black Friday restock.

With GTA 6 launching on Nov. 19 and Black Friday 2026 taking place on Nov. 27, there's a relatively short gap between the release date and the biggest shopping event of the year. With that, retailers could receive more inventory around launch or hold additional units for the holiday shopping period. However, neither possibility is guaranteed, especially for the black controller, which remains exclusive to PlayStation Direct in the U.S.

Even if there were more stock in time for Black Friday, it doesn't necessarily mean these special editions will be discounted. Given their limited availability and high demand, retailers have no reason to knock anything off the price just yet. At this stage, selling out at MSRP is already almost a guarantee.

For now, the safest approach is to keep checking the retailer pages above and secure your preferred design if it becomes available at a reasonable price. Waiting until Black Friday might pay off, but there's a slim chance that either controller will still be available to buy after Nov. 19.

#295. Lucid CEO says the company didn't know where its own cars were

Gaps in its logistics system caused the EV maker to lose orders.

Lucid, the California-based Tesla rival that makes luxury EVs like the Air and Gravity, has an interesting excuse for its third quarter earnings underperformance. The company said it lost sales due to inventory control issues, with CEO Silvio Napoli admitting in an earnings call that sometimes "we did not even know where cars were." That chaos resulted in slow delivery times up to two weeks that caused Lucid to lose orders from impatient buyers.

The company doesn't have millions of cars to keep track of like Tesla, either. Lucid built just 2,954 vehicles last quarter and delivered 3,806, down seven percent from the year before. Building fewer cars than it sold helped it reduce inventory, but it still had $1.22 billion worth on the books at the end of September.

With its logistics issues, Lucid's sales suffered like Tesla's did when the latter started ramping up the Model 3. Elon Musk's company had to take drastic measures to solve supply issues (like building its own car carriers and buying trucking companies), but once it did, sales took off.

Lucid's Napoli suggested that its latest fixes to shipping problems will have a similar effect by allowing the commercial team to "sell the cars that we know can be available on the day for the customers who require it." With that, along with a growing US EV market due to high gas prices (plus a deal it recently cut with robotaxi company Bolt), the company has positive expectations for the latter half of 2026.

Fixing its supply chain won't solve Lucid's other problems, though, like quality control issues and customer service complaints. The other tiny issue is that the automaker lost $831 million last quarter on revenue of just $350 million, due to cost of making each vehicle that far exceeds the selling price. However, that didn't discourage the company's primary investor, the Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF), from injecting an additional $1.75 billion into Lucid.
