#227. OpenAI, Anthropic face new oversight as New York’s AI safety law takes effect

New York is gearing up to enforce one of the country’s most significant AI safety laws. The state opened a registration portal Tuesday, with which large frontier AI developers like Anthropic and OpenAI will be required to register by January 1, 2027.

The RAISE Act, New York’s safety and transparency bill, kicks in at the start of the year. It mandates that large frontier developers — companies with more than $500 million in annual revenue — have to publish safety frameworks on their websites, report critical safety incidents within 72 hours, and issue public model transparency reports.

The newly formed Office of Digital Innovation, Governance, Integrity, and Trust (DIGIT) will implement the RAISE Act and oversee large frontier AI labs. Penalties can be as high as $1 million for one violation and $3 million for subsequent violations.

The state also can bring enforcement actions against companies that fail to register with the new portal, though a spokesperson declined to provide specifics.

“Tech moguls tell us that their vision for AI is inevitable,” Governor Kathy Hochul said Tuesday during a press briefing. “They tell us get on board or get left behind… That any collateral damage is worth it as long as we win the race to the AI cliff.”

On Tuesday, Hochul also launched the New York Center for AI Responsibility and Research based at State University of New York’s Binghamton University. The center will independently evaluate the safety, security, and operation of advanced AI systems and work with DIGIT to review companies’ safety frameworks. Hochul first announced the center in January with $55 million in donations and capital.

“We’re building a national model for smart, sensible AI regulation, a model that puts communities above corporations and people above powerful technology,” Hochul said.

The RAISE Act isn’t the only safety law New York has passed. Last year, the state began enforcing a law that requires AI companion operators to regularly let users know they’re talking to a bot, and to make reasonable efforts to detect suicidal ideation and self-harm.

In October, Hochul signed a separate chatbot safety law for minors, restricting behaviors like deceptive human-like interactions, emotionally manipulative or sycophantic responses, and encouragement of behaviors like suicide or disordered eating. That law takes effect June 1, 2027.

Other states have also passed similar AI safety and transparency bills to the RAISE Act. California’s SB 53, which took effect in January, mandates that frontier AI labs have in place and publish safety frameworks, enact whistleblower protections, and report critical safety incidents within 15 days. Illinois’ SB 315, which will go into effect at the start of 2027, has similar requirements, plus includes independent safety compliance audits.

#226. I asked every TV manufacturer 17 questions about how they spy on you

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After LG was accused of spying through its TVs, I wanted to see how other TV companies were collecting and protecting our data.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Every TV company is spying on you, not just LG. Like other tech companies, they collect lots of information, and while some is necessary for products to work, not all of it is — and could include details you’d prefer to keep private. Many customers are concerned about how TVs and streaming devices might be spying: listening to what you say, taking screenshots of everything you watch, storing sensitive information on your TV, and sharing your data with the cloud.

Apple: Emily Ewing, public relations manager

Google: Alex García-Kummert, spokesperson

LG Electronics: Chris De Maria, director of public relations

Samsung: Frank Giancamilli, corporate communications senior manager

TCL: Rob Manfredo, head of communications and events

One month ago, I invited 11 companies, including Amazon, Apple, Google, Hisense, LG, Panasonic, Roku, Samsung, Sony, TCL, and Walmart’s Vizio to share those answers. We now have answers from the top three TV manufacturers as well as every major streaming device company. Some companies declined to answer. Below, you can see and compare how each company answered (or failed to answer) each question and then decide how you feel. (One company, Hisense, ignored our emails entirely.)

As you’ll see them say, some of this data collection is opt-in, and you can often opt out. We know that many TVs use automatic content recognition (ACR) to figure out potentially everything we’re watching, and use that information to tailor ads, gather audience metrics, and serve up content recommendations based on our viewing habits. But though it sometimes seems like it’s required during the cavalcade of agreements thrown at us during setup, you can generally stop ACR even after you’ve agreed to it.

Opting out might not be as effective as it should be, however. A recent Northeastern University study found that Walmart’s Vizio kept on sending data after an opt-out (Vizio told me it was “routine operational data used to support the Vizio experience,” not ACR), while traffic with both Samsung and Roku was limited, but not stopped completely.

Below are the answers to our 17 questions. You will notice that some companies would not answer yes or no. We’ve also noted where companies declined to answer at all or ignored the questions. If any company becomes willing to directly answer our questions, we’ll add their responses.

No. Fire TV includes voice functionality to help customers use their devices and get to content they love and fast, not to surveil homes. The microphones on our voice remotes are designed to only work when the mic button on the remote is physically pressed. And TVs with hands-free Alexa are designed to detect the wake word (and only the wake word) before connecting to Alexa. No audio is stored or sent to the cloud unless the device detects the wake word. Customers can also turn off the microphones at any time. Every Fire TV smart television that has microphones also has a physical button or switch that disables the microphone.

Apple TV 4K will use its far-field microphones and the Always On Processor strictly to listen for the Siri wake word. The Always On Processor does not process or act on any other sounds in the home. Users have full control over this and can turn off “Siri” or “Hey Siri” as a wake word, or disable Siri entirely. When using the Siri Remote, users must actively press and hold the microphone button for it to start listening.

If Google Assistant or Gemini is enabled or available, microphones (built-in hands-free ‘Hey Google’ or remote push-to-talk button) listen for activation triggers. Microphones remain in passive standby until triggered by hotword or button press.

Hisense did not respond to The Verge’s questions despite five attempts to make contact.

Voice recognition is off by default and operates only if the user has opted in by accepting the Voice Information Agreement. Even if the user has opted in, they need to take additional steps for the microphone to be used. They must either press the microphone button on the remote control or they can use Hands-free Voice Control on supported models.

Hands-free Voice Control is disabled unless a user opts into this feature and then uses a wake-word. Wake-word detection is performed locally on the TV. If no wake word is detected, the audio is processed locally, promptly deleted, and is not stored, converted to text, or transmitted.

Panasonic did not provide an answer to this question.

While Roku TVs don’t have built-in microphones, some Roku remotes do. Standard voice remotes listen only while the mic button is held, and hands-free remotes can be switched off.

No, for private monitoring. Samsung TVs do not listen to or record private conversations.

The TV’s embedded microphone only listens locally for the specific wake-up word (like “Hi, Bixby”) and ignores all other sounds, while the remote control microphone only activates when the customer physically presses the button.

Sony did not provide an answer to this question.

Vizio did not provide an answer to this question.

Apple did not provide a direct answer to this question.

[Recorded audio and/or transcripts based on audio are] stored only if ‘Voice & Audio Activity’ (VAA) is explicitly enabled under Google Account Web & App Activity. Voice & Audio Activity is off by default. If VAA is off, voice requests are processed in real-time and not saved to the Google Account.

Hisense did not respond to The Verge’s questions despite five attempts to make contact.

If voice recognition is enabled (by opting in) and activated (by pushing the remote control’s microphone button or using the wake word), certain information generated as part of a specific voice recognition interaction may be stored locally on the TV to support voice-related features. Depending on the interaction, this may include Speech-to-text results derived from a user’s voice command.

Audio used for wake-word detection is processed locally on the TV and, if no wake word is detected, captured audio is promptly deleted, and is not stored, converted to text, or transmitted.

Panasonic did not provide an answer to this question.

No. Samsung Smart TVs do not store voice recordings or transcripts on the device.

However, on secure servers, voice commands and transcripts are stored only to process requests and improve voice assistant services.

Sony did not provide an answer to this question.

TCL does not collect audio data from TCL TV or remote microphones, or from the use of third-party voice features.

Vizio did not provide an answer to this question.

When customers speak to Alexa by pressing the mic button on the remote or using their chosen wake word, a recording of the request is sent to Amazon’s cloud, where a text transcript of the request is created to help Alexa provide a response. No audio is captured without the customer invoking Alexa—either by pressing the mic button or saying the wake word—and customers can easily review exactly what they asked and what Alexa heard, delete their recording, or even choose not to save them at all.

For moments when users ask a question that requires up‑to‑date information, Siri AI can go out to the web and find it, then use Private Cloud Compute to generate an answer. Searches and requests are associated with a random identifier — a long string of letters and numbers — not a user’s Apple Account.

If Voice & Audio Activity is turned on, when voice features are activated (via hotword or remote button), captured audio or transcripts are transmitted to Google cloud servers for processing and fulfilling commands. If VAA is turned off, these are deleted.

Hisense did not respond to The Verge’s questions despite five attempts to make contact.

Yes, but only if voice recognition has been enabled (by opting in) and activated (by pushing the remote control microphone button or using the wake word). When voice recognition is activated, the TV displays an on-screen indication that voice recognition is active and listening for the user’s command. Once the user finishes the voice command, the command is transmitted to our server, where it is converted into text and processed to respond to the command.

Panasonic did not provide an answer to this question.

Sony did not provide an answer to this question.

TCL did not provide a direct answer to this question.

Vizio did not provide an answer to this question.

Apple TV 4K does not use automatic content recognition (ACR), nor does it collect screenshots, screen recordings, or video recordings of what viewers are watching. If a user chooses to ask a question about what’s on their screen, that data is processed by Siri AI. This feature is only initiated by a direct user request.

Google TV platform software does not utilize Automatic Content Recognition (ACR) to capture on-screen video or screenshots. (Note: Third-party TV hardware OEMs may install separate ACR software, but Google TV OS itself does not).

Hisense did not respond to The Verge’s questions despite five attempts to make contact.

No. ACR in LG TVs does not collect or store screenshots, screen images, screen recordings, or video recordings from the TV. Instead, it uses audio fingerprints generated from content audio using the TV’s internal soundboard (not a speaker) to identify the content being viewed.

Panasonic did not provide an answer to this question.

No. Samsung TVs do not capture or store screenshots, screen recordings, or video recordings of what viewers watch.

Sony did not provide an answer to this question.

TCL did not provide a direct answer to this question.

Vizio did not provide an answer to this question.

Apple did not provide a direct answer to this question.

[Google TV] stores and transmits viewing history, watchlists, and app interactions for rentals/purchases on GTV and FreePlay. We do not have viewing history on third party apps.

Hisense did not respond to The Verge’s questions despite five attempts to make contact.

If the user has agreed to the Viewing Information Agreement, viewing information may be collected or processed in certain circumstances, including the following:

- ACR: Where ACR is available and enabled, ACR-related information may be collected and processed to identify content being viewed from supported sources. ACR uses audio fingerprinting technology to identify content rather than collecting screenshots, screen recordings, video recordings, voice recordings, or other audio recordings from the TV. To operate ACR services, LG may process content-identification fingerprints together with device information, content source information, network information, and consent settings needed to support the feature. ACR is optional and is turned off by default, and users can disable the feature at any time through the TV settings. ACR does not operate within third-party streaming apps.

- LG Channels: When a user streams content through LG Channels, information about the content being viewed is processed and sent to our servers so we can provide the requested streaming service.

- Voice Recognition: When users make voice commands, those commands and current viewing information (such as channel number and program name, and whether the source is an antenna or set-top-box) are sent to the server to process the voice commands and provide appropriate responses.

- Usage Information: Certain viewing information may also be collected as part of usage information relating to how the user interacts with the TV and its services, such as information about content selected for viewing or channels the user switches to.

Panasonic did not provide an answer to this question.

Yes, but only for TV programming and app viewing history. Samsung TVs do not monitor or transmit your personal files or private media.

Sony did not provide an answer to this question.

Vizio did not provide an answer to this question.

Apple did not provide an answer to this question.

[Google TV] collects device navigation telemetry (button presses, navigation clicks, app launch/exit events) under ‘Usage & Diagnostics’ settings to assess system health and reliability. Does not store “hovering” or “highlight” states.

LG does not collect information about a user’s activity within third-party apps. LG does not track where users are looking when using the TV.

Yes. For example, IP addresses are transmitted as part of normal network communication, for your device to communicate with the internet and deliver content. There is no GPS hardware on Fire TV devices.

If the TV is connected to the internet, then like any other internet-connected device, the Smart TV will transmit its IP address. The IP address may be used to determine its approximate geographic location to provide services appropriate for the user’s region.

Separately, when ACR is enabled, the TV’s IP address and information about nearby Wi-Fi networks, such as MAC addresses and signal strength, may be used to estimate the TV’s approximate location as part of the ACR service. This ACR-related location processing is limited to ACR and is not used generally across other TV services.

The TV does not contain a GPS module and does not use GPS to determine its location.

Panasonic did not provide an answer to this question.

No, for physical locations. Samsung TVs do not collect or transmit GPS coordinates, Wi-Fi SSIDs, or your physical street address. This IP address is only used for network communication and general region detection (like country or state), not to pinpoint your actual address.

#225. OpenAI acquires Odessia, travel startup created by Sonder founder

OpenAI has acquired an AI travel agent called Odessia, according to Odessia’s founder, Francis Davidson. Davidson says in a post announcing the deal that he’s now working for OpenAI, helping the AI lab with the future of travel.

Terms of the deal were not disclosed, but this looks like an acqui-hire. Davidson’s announcement focused largely on how he built his AI travel agent Odessia by directing teams of AI agents. The year-old Odessia raised a $6 million pre-seed round led by Sequoia and launched its AI travel agent around five months ago. Davidson says that it had helped early adopters plan millions of dollars’ worth of travel, but the tool has already been shut down.

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the hire to TechCrunch. “We’re thrilled to welcome Francis to OpenAI. Francis is a proven founder who has built multiple businesses to help people travel, and we’re excited he’s chosen to build with us.”

However the AI lab has not announced new travel-specific products or plans. It does, however, count such companies as Bookings.com, Expedia, and KAYAK as customers or partners. Travel is also one of the main areas that people are using personal AI assistants to help with. And, back in July 2025, when OpenAI launched its ChatGPT agent, it mentioned travel as one of its potential uses.

OpenAI’s spokesperson didn’t have more to share on its plans for travel-related products, except to say that travel is one of the areas that chatbot users frequently ask about. “1.2 billion people turn to ChatGPT each week for help with everyday tasks — from understanding their health, figuring out what to make for dinner, building a budget, and even planning a trip. We see a real opportunity to make these experiences even more useful,” the spokesperson said.

Acquiring Odessia is interesting for another reason. Davidson is well known in the travel industry after he founded a travel startup called Sonder as a college student in 2014. It was sort of like Airbnb, but it focused on short-term leasing of upscale apartments in major cities worldwide. The pandemic badly hurt it, and as it climbed out of that rut, it went public via a SPAC but didn’t raise as much money as it needed, Davidson later said in a post announcing his resignation as CEO in June 2025.

When a key partnership with Marriott fell through, the company went into bankruptcy and wound down. The bankruptcy trustee sold Sonder’s name, trademarks, and website domain in 2026 to TravelAI, and that website operates as a curated guide to the kind of travel rental properties that Sonder was once known for. Prior to its downfall, Davidson had built Sonder into a unicorn with $143 million in revenues. While Sonder was not, ultimately, a success story, Davidson is the kind of battle-scarred founder that Silicon Valley often values.

So we’ll have to wait and see what OpenAI has in store now with travel, now that he’s joined the company.

Note: This story was updated with OpenAI’s comments.

#223. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.

#221. Streaming is still trying to be TikTok

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It hasn’t proven that short-form video will help.

It hasn’t proven that short-form video will help.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

At Skydance’s first employee town hall after it acquired Warner Bros. Discovery in October, CEO David Ellison told staff that the company behind Paramount Plus and HBO Max was competing for people’s attention with some of the world’s biggest tech companies. Months earlier, he’d singled out a way to get viewers to spend more time with Paramount’s programming. A feed of short vertical videos, he said, was “critical to our future.”

On Skydance’s earnings call Monday, Ellison put a name to the competition. Add up all of Paramount’s and Warner Bros. Discovery’s broadcast, cable, and streaming properties, he said, and they still trail YouTube in US television watch time, according to Nielsen. Ellison said Paramount Plus engagement had risen more than 20 percent since Skydance acquired Paramount last year. He didn’t mention how the clips feed panned out.

On YouTube, a few clips can be the whole visit. YouTube accounted for a record 15.8 percent of viewing through US TV sets in August, according to Nielsen. YouTube also leads in time spent watching video on phones, where it competes with TikTok and Instagram, says Ross Benes, a senior analyst at eMarketer. The firm estimates that US adults spend a little over an hour and a half a day watching video on mobile devices and about two and a half hours on connected TVs. It counts simultaneous phone and TV viewing in both totals.

The streamers have plenty of shows to clip, but they’re up against social apps with millions of people making something new reacting to that existing content every day. A scene in a streaming app is a pitch for the full episode. On TikTok or YouTube, a creator’s reaction to that same episode can be the reason you open the app in the first place. There’s something to come back for, even when the show is between seasons.

On Disney Plus, the company is turning to fan-made videos in a more direct bid to compete with social video. In August, Disney announced a US pilot with TikTok to bring clips about its movies and shows into Verts. The feed will feature a curated selection from creators who opt in, and the clips will appear on both apps. On Disney’s August earnings call, CEO Josh D’Amaro said the deal could make Disney Plus a “stickier app” where people stay longer and discover more, which “benefits our whole ecosystem.” An internal roadmap reviewed by Business Insider targets early 2027 for adding creator content and calls for sports and news clips and short-form series such as microdramas.

Disney has shown that people will use a clips feed inside a streaming app. The next question is whether the feed becomes a reason to open the app in the first place, and another reason to keep paying for it.

Paramount is looking beyond its own catalog, too. After adding a clips feed to Paramount Plus in April, it was exploring user-generated video for a possible free tier by September. On Paramount’s May earnings call, Ellison said viewers were going from clips to news, sports, and entertainment, without saying how often.

HBO Max began testing Shorts with select US iPhone users in July, letting viewers jump straight from a clip into a movie or show. Skydance’s third-quarter results excluded HBO Max because the quarter ended six days before the Warner Bros. Discovery deal closed. The company gave no usage figures for either feed on its November call.

At Netflix, Jefferies estimates that average monthly viewing fell from 66 hours per member in the first half of 2023 to 49 in the first half of 2026. On Netflix’s October earnings call, co-CEO Greg Peters pointed to lower cancellation rates in the US and globally as evidence that engagement remained healthy.

On the same call, co-CEO Ted Sarandos downplayed the role of short-form video six months after Netflix made Clips a centerpiece of its mobile redesign. He grouped short-form video with other early experiments and said Netflix’s core entertainment business still accounted for roughly 95 percent of spending and nearly all revenue. Netflix plans to roll out themed Clips feeds in 2027, but it hasn’t said whether Clips gets people to watch more.

Making money from the feed itself is harder. Benes says ads on phones generally fetch less than ads on TVs, and a short clip has less room for ad breaks than a movie or an episode. “I just don’t see it being like a big success for them,” he says. He sees a more plausible role for these feeds as something to scroll through on your phone while the TV is on.

Streamers may end up borrowing something useful from TikTok: a better way to browse. Their business still depends on what people settle in to watch.

#220. X launches its own clipping feature to spur influencer engagement

Over the past few years, X has released a number of features to make itself a friendlier hub for influencers. The latest effort to attract social media talent? Its own clipping tool.

Clipping is the pivotal social media technique by which longer videos are spliced into TikTok-attention-span length “clips” and then strategically posted to engage as many eyeballs (and thus derive as much ad revenue) as possible.

On Monday, X announced the tool, which, from what we can tell, appears to be little more than a straightforward video-trimming feature that lets users edit videos and post them to X.

The site says that every clip includes a “Clip from” label that links back to the original video from which it was derived, allowing X users to see more about the creator and the creator’s content.

“Grab your favorite moment from any video, add your take, and post it without leaving X,” the company posted.

The platform added that both creators and clippers can “earn from views on the clip through Original Content Rewards,” X’s rewards program. The program is only available for eligible creators and pays out based on specific levels of audience engagement.

In many cases, influencers pay special firms to handle clipping for them. More and more large companies are also turning to such services to run marketing campaigns, and the technique has been used in political campaigns as well.

#216. Nvidia’s crisis has corporate governance experts reaching for the popcorn

Nvidia’s stock sank 5.47% on Tuesday, following a 4% drop on Monday, a loss of about 9.5% in two days, even though there wasn’t much news out of the company. CEO Jensen Huang has reportedly been trying to conduct business as usual after admitting to an extramarital affair with Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence agencies believe was spying for China. The biggest development: the board hired the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an internal investigation.

What comes next is mostly a guessing game, even for the people who study how boards are supposed to handle crises. Two corporate governance professors, Ann Lipton of the University of Colorado and Todd Henderson of the University of Chicago, say there is no real precedent for a CEO caught up in a suspected honeypot. And while they have views on what the investigation should ask, like everyone else, they’re watching to see how it plays out.

“There have actually been affair cases and sexual harassment cases,” Lipton said, but was quick to add that she doesn’t know of previous cases that also involve potential national security compromises. Henderson, whose research interests include corporations, securities regulation, and law and economics, said much depends on facts few people have yet, including, most importantly, what, if anything, Alice Huang got out of Jensen Huang. (Secrets about Nvidia’s fabs or chip designs would matter to the Justice Department. Ordinary corporate information probably would not.)

Lipton described the investigation led by Nvidia’s independent board members, with their own outside counsel, as perhaps the most “standard” aspect of the whole affair so far. She also observed that an investigation will be “exactly as thorough” as the directors running it want it to be, which is also standard. Henderson made a related point about incentives, saying that Washington law firms that conduct internal investigations “know who pays their bills.” He didn’t suggest that’s happening here.

Lipton said she sees three lines of inquiry, including whether the law was broken, whether Huang violated Nvidia’s internal policies, and whether the board was misled, including how forthcoming he was and when he was on notice. It’s notable, as Wired reported Monday, that Huang hired a PR crisis firm known for handling sex scandals a full year ago. Of course, the driver could have been about the affair alone. It’s unclear when Huang learned Alice Huang was a suspected spy. The board said in a Sunday statement about the matter that Nvidia has “has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised.”

Henderson similarly stressed the importance of what Huang knew and when. He described two scenarios. In one, Huang hires a PR firm because an extramarital affair is embarrassing, without knowing who the woman is. In the other, he knows she is a suspected Chinese spy. “I think that’s very different,” he said. “If I were a board member and the CEO of a company with very sensitive technology was in that situation, I would be furious if they didn’t tell me.” Not telling the board that you have cancer “or that you’ve been sleeping with a Chinese spy,” said Henderson, “seems to me to be a fireable offense.”

Either way, Lipton expects shareholder suits, and she said two kinds are possible. Securities fraud, which she said is “only about lying,” is the most obvious, and as we reported yesterday, Nvidia is already facing a proposed class action lawsuit that alleges Nvidia and Huang misled investors by concealing Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang while describing dealings with her former employer as routine. “You can have as many affairs with Chinese spies as you like,” Lipton said. “You just can’t lie about it under the federal securities laws.”

A mismanagement claim under Delaware law, where Nvidia is incorporated, is much harder. Employees do “stupid or reckless or damaging things all the time,” she said, and courts don’t let shareholders sue each time. Shareholders bringing such a claim on the company’s behalf, known as a derivative suit, must first show the board is incapable of handling the problem, usually by proving directors are implicated or so beholden to the CEO that they can’t act independently.

Nvidia’s 10-member board has a track record of loyalty and deference to Huang, as outlined by a New York Times piece Tuesday morning. Still, the investigation those “independent” directors have announced with outside counsel makes that very hard. Lipton cited, for example, a shareholder suit over alleged mismanagement at McDonald’s several years ago, after its chief people officer was accused of harassment and its CEO had a relationship with a subordinate. Ultimately, the court found no reason the board couldn’t deal with the misconduct on its own.

Even if none of this ends up threatening Huang’s job, the episode raises other governance questions that don’t look great in retrospect. Nvidia is run with a famously flat structure, with more than 60 direct reports to Huang, and the company doesn’t appear to have a succession plan, which Lipton called “a danger for any company.”

Huang’s children also work at Nvidia. His son Spencer is a director of product management, his daughter Madison is a senior director of product marketing, and his son-in-law, Nico Caprez, works in business development. Some employees believe he is grooming successors, and asked last month by Bloomberg whether Madison is in line for the job, Huang didn’t shoot down the idea, saying instead that it’s not something he’d wish on her.

Lipton called hiring the kids poor corporate hygiene, even though founders have long done it. (Kimbal Musk is on Tesla’s board because he’s Elon Musk’s brother, she noted.) Directors are supposed to act only for stockholders, but founders get more rope as long as the company is thriving.

Henderson similarly calls himself a strong “anti-nepotism person,” sharing that when his father worked at Westinghouse’s nuclear research lab, there was an absolute ban on hiring employees’ children. “Like, fine for them to be working there,” he said of Huang’s children working at Nvidia. But “the idea that one of his kids would succeed him” at a publicly traded company the size of Nvidia “seems crazy,” he added.

What Nvidia may have gotten more right, from a governance standpoint, is what it doesn’t have, which is dual-class shares. Huang reportedly owns about 3.6%, so he doesn’t control the vote. That makes him “easier to dislodge,” Lipton said, even though he is deeply integrated into the company in other ways.

Neither professor is rooting for or against Huang, each made clear. The simple truth is that with voting control on top of Huang’s holdings, the board would have even less flexibility. Without it, the board has a few more options.

#215. Nvidia hires leading US law firm to investigate Jensen Huang’s relationship

Move comes as chipmaker reels from reports its CEO was having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy

Nvidia has appointed a leading US law firm to conduct a “thorough and independent” investigation into the relationship between its chief executive, Jensen Huang, and an alleged Chinese spy.

The $4.1tn (£3tn) chipmaker has hired New York-based Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz for the investigation, which will be led by the Wachtell partner Joon Kim. Wachtell’s website describes Kim, a former government prosecutor in New York, as a specialist in areas including internal investigations, white-collar criminal and regulatory defence, and crisis management.

Nvidia did not give a timeframe for the investigation but the company has said Huang will continue to lead the business he co-founded “without interruption”.

The company is reeling from an espionage scandal after reports that its chief executive had been having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, who was also an Nvidia customer.

The US ambassador to China, David Perdue, said: “The situation with Nvidia is another reminder that we have to stay diligent.

“National security is part of this economic conversation because economic security is national security. And so, what we’re doing with China is bumpy right now,” he told Bloomberg Television.

Nvidia had at first denied an improper relationship with the Chinese citizen, who is no relation to the Nvidia boss, but Huang then admitted on Sunday he had been unfaithful to his wife – without referring directly to Ms Huang.

On the same day Nvidia announced an investigation into reports that Huang “has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort”. The scandal has wiped $430bn off Nvidia’s value as shareholders gauge the potential impact of the scandal on a company and an executive who are synonymous with the AI boom.

Wachtell is one of the most prestigious corporate law firms in the US. Its founding partner Martin Lipton invented the “poison pill defence” in the 1980s, a corporate gambit that helps companies ward off hostile takeovers.

Technically called the “shareholder rights plan”, the poison pill allowed investors to buy more shares at a discount if one shareholder acquired a certain percentage of the company’s stock. This made it harder for corporate raiders to acquire a company without the board’s approval.

The firm was closely involved in the takeover of Getty Oil by Texaco. This was the largest corporate takeover in history at the time, although it turned sour when the thwarted suitor Pennzoil sued Texaco and won.

In 2008, a team of Wachtell lawyers advised the US Treasury department on the takeover of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac during the financial crisis.

The company was founded in the 1960s and is said to be the most profitable law firm in the US. It pulled in revenues of $5.1m per lawyer last year and made profits of $12m per partner, the highest profits per partner ever, according to the Am Law 100 rankings.

From 2017 to 2018, Kim was the acting US attorney for the southern district of New York, where he ran a team of lawyers investigating securities and commodities fraud, mail and wire fraud, public and private corruption, money laundering, cybercrime, and tax offences.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

That included the case of the former US representative Anthony Weiner, who was sentenced to 21 months in prison for sending obscene material to a minor.

He also oversaw the prosecution of the banker Mehmet Hakan Atilla, who was convicted in 2017 of helping to evade US sanctions on Iran.

Another high-profile assignment landed on Kim’s desk in 2021, when he investigated multiple allegations of sexual harassment by the then New York governor, Andrew Cuomo.

One analyst has put Huang’s chances of leaving Nvidia at 15%.

“Right now the Street and investor chatter is assigning roughly a 15% chance he is removed as CEO of Nvidia with a high probability (85%) he stays on as CEO after the board investigation is completed,” wrote the tech analyst Dan Ives in a note to investors. “We would expect a board investigation that could take four weeks to six weeks and will create a clear overhang on the stock until more news emerges from Nvidia.”

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, the senior associate dean for leadership studies at the Yale School of Management and the founder of the Chief Executive Leadership Institute, has put Huang’s survival chances at “about 50-50”.

#213. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#210. New Orleans attorney fined $400,000 after exposing abusive priest says it was ‘worth it’

Richard Trahant says accusers have been vindicated after Catholic church officially classifies Paul Hart as sex abuser

An attorney who was fined more than $400,000 after getting an abusive Roman Catholic priest removed as a New Orleans high school’s chaplain says it was “worth it” now that the church has officially classified the cleric as a clergy molester.

That remark from Richard Trahant came days after the archdiocese of New Orleans, having waited years, added Paul Hart on 6 November to a list of Catholic priests and deacons faced with credible claims of clergy abuse while serving in the area.

The late Hart had previously dismissed news coverage of his admitted but largely unpunished sexual misconduct with a 17-year-old girl he met in the course of his ministry as “bogus” and “made-up”. But, while continuing to implement the terms of an agreement to settle a bankruptcy protection case that it filed amid the fallout of the Catholic church’s worldwide clergy abuse scandal, the New Orleans archdiocese ultimately included Hart on a list of more than 100 credibly accused priests and deacons – vindicating those who spoke out against him and his religious superiors.

“This is the very definition of a Pyrrhic victory,” Trahant said, invoking a phrase for something achieved at a high cost. “Sure, it’s exoneration – yet only after I paid a fortune.

“But if the question is: was it worth it? The answer is yes, and I would do it again tomorrow.”

Hart and Trahant were at the center of one of the most contentious chapters of the New Orleans archdiocese’s bankruptcy, which the institution filed in 2020.

Trahant’s work representing clergy abuse claimants whose cases became ensnared in the archdiocesan bankruptcy gave him access to documents chronicling how Hart had secretly admitted to his supervisors that he had sexual contact with a 17-year-old girl in the early 1990s after meeting her through his role as a priest.

In 2012, she reported Hart to the church for that behavior and confessed to it during a confidential internal investigation, according to hundreds of legal documents obtained and reviewed by the Guardian.

An advisory board working with New Orleans’s archbishop at the time, Gregory Aymond, recommended removing Hart from public ministry, saying the case victimized someone who was considered a minor under canon – or church – law since 2002.

But Aymond let Hart continue his ministry because in the early 1990s the age of consent under canon law was 16. And Louisiana law sets the age of consent at 17.

Aymond then appointed Hart to serve as chaplain at Brother Martin after the New Orleans Catholic high school asked for one in 2017.

“Please know that I not only appreciate your ministry but will pray for you that God will use you to be a sign of pastoral care to our young people,” Aymond wrote to Hart after appointing him to Brother Martin, an all boys’ school where there are activities that girls can participate in.

Hart was still in that role toward the end of 2021 when Trahant became aware of his past. He notified Brother Martin’s principal – coincidentally, Trahant’s cousin – that Hart had “a credible allegation from [his] past that involved a minor”.

Also, Trahant emailed this journalist to keep Hart on his “radar”. But he would not elaborate to either Gallagher or this journalist, citing a bankruptcy court confidentiality order, according to the legal documents stemming from the case.

Aymond and the archdiocese soon provided Brother Martin with the details about Hart’s past misconduct. And he retired in January 2022, nine months before his death, after Brother Martin requested that Aymond remove him from its campus.

Meanwhile, using methods and sources exclusive of Trahant, this journalist wrote an article in New Orleans’s Times-Picayune newspaper detailing the circumstances of Hart’s departure – though the archdiocese insisted the priest had retired because of “struggles with treatment for brain cancer”.

The federal judge in charge of the archdiocese’s bankruptcy, Meredith Grabill, later ruled what Trahant had done set off a chain of events that was enough to constitute a confidentiality order violation. She fined him $400,000 – which eventually grew by $60,000 because of interest. And she expelled four of Trahant’s clients from a committee of clergy abuse survivors helping negotiate an archdiocesan bankruptcy settlement, contending that it would aid the group’s functioning.

Grabill so acted despite a court investigation which she ordered having determined that Brother Martin got the specifics about what Hart had done from the archdiocese – not Trahant, according to documents obtained by the Guardian.

Court investigators also conceded there was evidence supporting Trahant’s denial that he ever provided any confidential information to this journalist.

Trahant unsuccessfully appealed his fine from Grabill up to the US supreme court. But the supreme court declined to hear his case in June, months after Aymond’s retirement as New Orleans archbishop – so he paid the levy.

Aymond’s successor, archbishop James Checchio, on 6 November then added 27 priests and deacons to a list of credibly accused clergymen which the New Orleans archdiocese first released in 2018, calling it a step toward “accountability and healing”. The list now had 106 names after the update – a key provision of an agreement announced in December 2025 requiring the archdiocese and its insurers to eventually pay $305m to hundreds of clergy abuse survivors to resolve the bankruptcy.

Hart’s name being listed immediately leapt out at Trahant, he said.

The archdiocese said that records associated with Hart and all others on the credibly accused list would be kept at a public archive hosted by Louisiana State University. A release of such records for Hart would mean none of the information in them was ever confidential, according to Trahant.

Trahant also said he interpreted Hart’s inclusion on the credibly accused list – and a notation that he had three claims filed against him in the bankruptcy – as an admission that archdiocese and court officials had mishandled his case. Furthermore, he said it was perhaps the ultimate repudiation of one of Hart’s last known statements – telling bankruptcy court investigators that news media reporting on his departure from Brother Martin was “so bogus that it looked … almost made up”.

“He was lying,” said Trahant, mentioning that he is contemplating seeking further legal action out of principle to attempt to recoup his fine and the costs he incurred fighting it. “And … now they’ve admitted [it].”

Separately, one of the Trahant clients who was ousted from the bankruptcy settlement committee said Checchio could “truly” promote accountability by apologizing to the advocate and returning the fine money to him.

“At least [now], everyone knows that we were wrongly removed,” James Adams said. “The court sanctioned an innocent lawyer and his clients.”

#206. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.

#203. US could lose measles-free status soon as RFK Jr’s pushes anti-vaccine agenda

Country is in the middle of worst measles outbreak since 1991, with more than 5,400 cases nationally

The United States could lose its measles elimination status this week, an event likely to cement health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr’s legacy of meddling in vaccine policies amid the worst outbreak of the virus in the US in 35 years.

If the US loses its measles-free status, it would join Canada and several European and Asian nations that had eliminated the disease only for it to return.

“That is a tremendous step backwards,” said Dr Paul Offit, director of the Vaccine Education Center, an attending physician in the division of infectious diseases at Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia, and a prominent critic of Kennedy.

A panel of eight independent experts will consider the epidemiological evidence in Washington DC, part of a group called the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a regional branch of the World Health Organization (WHO). The PAHO director will make the final call.

“Independent of what PAHO says, measles is now endemic in the US. Measles has a foothold in the US,” said Offit. “We’re going to see it this year, we’re going to see it next year,” because the US “critically decreased the percentage” of people vaccinated.

The US is in the midst of its worst measles outbreak since 1991 with more than 5,400 cases nationally, according to the Johns Hopkins University International Vaccine Access Center tracker. Just this year, six people have died in Pennsylvania, a child died in New York on election day and an infant died in Wisconsin days later. Nearly every US state has an active outbreak.

Measles is considered one of the most infectious diseases known to medicine. The overwhelming majority of Americans vaccinate themselves and their children, but the sheer contagiousness of measles means even small dips in immunity allow it to spread. It kills one to three people per 1,000 people and hospitalizes about one-in-five unvaccinated people who are infected. Two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is 97% effective against the disease.

Although elimination status is a formal public health designation, its loss has real effects on everyday Americans. In just one example, Dr David Margolius, the Cleveland, Ohio,director of public health, is warning people to find proof of their measles vaccination in the event they’re exposed to a measles-positive patient.

“Now is the time to go and find that evidence, because now this is a real day-to-day life thing that could happen to you,” said Margolius, who is also a director-at-large of the Big Cities Health Coalition. Without proof, patients face either an immunology blood draw or a 21-day quarantine.

Cleveland health authorities have already done a tremendous amount of detective work this year to track down people exposed to measles patients in hospital waiting rooms and elevators.

“We reach out to people and for most people, including myself, we have no personal experience with measles,” he said. “They’ve got everything else going on in their life, and now they’re getting this call.”

The types of people who contract measles has also changed. In 1963, when the vaccine was first licensed, measles was considered a childhood disease. Most children contracted the disease by the age of five.

“Now you’re seeing the epidemiology shift,” said Offit. Two-thirds of people infected with measles in Pennsylvania are adults, a group on which researchers have little information. Mothers have even given birth to babies with measles. “Now, you’re seeing something we just didn’t see at all – congenital measles.”

The potential loss of elimination status comes at a tenuous moment for the Trump administration. Republicans just endured a historic drubbing in the midterm elections. Several new Democratic doctors and scientists are slated to come into Congress on promises to hold Kennedy accountable. At the same time, Trump has doubled down on his commitment to vaccine antagonism with the appointment of Stephen Miller as chief of staff.

“Next to me, Stephen Miller is the strongest Maha supporter in the administration,” Kennedy said in a statement about Miller’s appointment. “It’s time to unleash the Kraken.”

Meanwhile, the capacity of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) to respond has been significantly hampered by the Trump administration. The agency has lost up to one-third of its staff, Kennedy has interfered in the agency’s reporting of measles deaths and has spread misinformation about the measles vaccines.

Even so, Margolius doesn’t lay all the blame at Kennedy’s feet. Vaccine hesitancy was increasing before Trump took office, and pandemic-era disruptions were real.

“Some of the narrative has been to pin this on our health secretary, which certainly I think he could do much more to help the situation rather than hurting the situation, but I do think the case count is climbing everywhere,” he said. “This could happen even under a more pro-public health administration.”

The second Trump administration also made broad changes that challenge measles elimination globally. Some of Trump’s first acts in 2024 included withdrawal from the World Health Organization (WHO) and the Global Vaccine Alliance (GAVI). GAVI provides measles vaccines to nations such as India and Malawi. There are about 95,000 measles deaths globally, according to the World Health Organization, mostly in unvaccinated children under five years old.

At home, Republicans also enacted the largest ever federal cut to health insurance, changes expected to result in more than 17 million people losing insurance through 2034. That has challenged people such as Margolius, who struggle to connect people with vaccines when their health insurance coverage is unstable.

“Until we can rejoin the WHO and cooperate with other health agencies all over the world ... This will continue,” said Margolius.

#202. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

#197. GOP branded ‘sinking ship’ as Republicans block efforts to impeach Hegseth – US politics live

Trump hails ‘exoneration of a Great American Patriot’ as moves against his defense secretary fail for now

House Republicans block effort to impeach Hegseth

Hello and welcome to the US politics live blog.

Republican representative Thomas Massie compared the GOP with passengers content to go down with the Titanic after colleagues thwarted his effort to impeach defense secretary Pete Hegseth.

The vote came a week after Republicans lost control of the Senate and House of Representatives in their worst midterm election in more than a half century, and underscored how lawmakers remain wary of crossing Donald Trump, who has signaled his continued support for Hegseth.

“They know it’s a sinking ship but committed to stay on board,” Massie, a Republican congressman who lost his primary after breaking with the president, said.

Top Democrats announced their support for the resolution, but Republicans moved to kill it by referring it to the judiciary committee. Their motion succeeded on Tuesday, by a 211-204 vote, largely along party lines. Massie was the sole Republican to oppose the motion, along with all Democrats.

Trump, who was twice impeached, celebrated what he hailed as the “EXONERATION of a Great American Patriot” and lashed out at Massie for forcing the vote.

“The Democrats continue to weaponize and abuse the United States of America,” Trump said on Truth Social. “They must be stopped!”

The Pentagon, meanwhile, declared “total victory”. “The referral of these baseless impeachment articles to the House judiciary committee marks total victory for Secretary Hegseth, the Department of War, and the American warfighter,” Sean Parnell, a department spokesperson, wrote in a social media post.

Some 11 Democratic representatives skipped the vote, the Daily Beast reported earlier this morning, causing the articles of impeachment to fail. Among those to miss the vote was Nancy Pelosi.

If eight of ‌them had voted with fellow Democrats the measure could have proceeded.

House Democratic leader Hakeen Jeffries said he would talk to members about whether to mount a new impeachment effort in the next ​Congress in January when the Democrats who triumphed in the midterm take their seats. “We’ll have that conversation at the appropriate time,” he said.

Congressman Jim Jordan, a Republican hardliner from Ohio, won the election to become the next House minority leader in Congress. Jordan beat Representative Steve Scalise, the current majority leader, by 106-73 in a closed-door vote to lead Republicans from January. He replaces the current House speaker, Mike Jonson, who said he would not run when the GOP cedes control to Democrats in the new year.

Meanwhile, Republicans in the upper chamber, re-elected John Thune to serve as Senate minority leader in the next Congress. The GOP senator from North Dakota didn’t face a challenger in the closed-door vote, as Democrats prepare for at least a 10-seat majority in the new year.

If there was one obvious conclusion from Democrats’ sweeping victories in the mid-term elections on Tuesday, it seemed to be that the Republican effort to redraw congressional districts in their favor backfired.

Starting last summer, Trump and Republican allies undertook an aggressive effort to redraw congressional districts, reconfiguring the lines to give Republican candidates an advantage. Ahead of the midterms, Republicans redrew 15 seats to their advantage. After Tuesday’s election, they appear poised to lose all but two of them. “It was definitely the wrong move,” an unnamed House Republican told Fox News. “One of the truisms in politics is never to overreach. We did and that was part of the reason for the backlash.”

The Republicans losses included defeats in Texas, Florida and North Carolina where Republicans hoped to pick up as many as 10 combined congressional seats.

“The maps themselves did fail, and failed quite epically,” said John Bisognano, the president of the National Democratic Redistricting Committee, a Democratic redistricting group. “I would also argue the idea itself was an even bigger failure. Allowing an environment to be created around you in which everyone begins to realize how deeply corrupt this administration is and how fearful they are of the American people in elections, so much so that their main tactical path forward is going to be to redraw maps to steal as many voters as they can.”

But even though Republicans failed to pick up nearly all of the new seats they drew, it would be misleading to say that gerrymandering did not work at all in this election, said Nicholas Stephanopoulos, a professor at Harvard who studies redistricting. Republicans didn’t pick up the seats because Democrats had the strongest midterm elections in half a century.
